After a tense faculty review where she reacted angrily to feedback and her partner at home reported that she hadn’t been sleeping and seemed uncharacteristically “amped up” and distractible, her department chair privately suggested she see occupational health, which then referred her for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear her work and relationships were significantly impaired.\n- Chief Complaint: “For months I’ve felt unusually ‘up’ and on edge all the time, like my mood is cranked way higher than normal and I can’t dial it back.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Lately it’s like someone wired me to a constant high voltage—I'm either euphoric and talking a mile a minute about my ideas, or I flip on a dime and snap at my students or my partner over tiny things, and I just can’t seem to bring the dial back to normal.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): For months I've been bouncing from the lab to my office to committee meetings, starting new experiments, drafting conference proposals, rewriting lectures all night, and I literally can’t sit still long enough to finish anything before I feel compelled to jump into the next ‘big’ thing.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I’ve been absolutely convinced that I’m on the verge of a field‑changing breakthrough, that only I can see the connections everyone else is missing, and when colleagues question me it feels ridiculous because in my mind there’s no doubt I’m operating on a different level than they are.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): Most nights I only sleep two or three hours, if that, but I wake up buzzing with ideas, pacing around making notes on the whiteboard at 3 a.m., and I don’t feel tired at all, just irritated that my body can’t keep up with how fast my mind is going.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): In meetings or in the lab my mind jumps everywhere—an email ping, a flickering light, a random thought about a new grant idea—and I suddenly abandon what I was doing to chase that distraction, so I have half‑finished notes, experiments, and conversations scattered all over the place.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Over the past several months my lab has slipped into chaos—deadlines have been missed, students have complained to the department about my erratic behavior, and my chair has warned that my promotion and even my position could be at risk, while at home my partner is exhausted and says I’m impossible to be around.\n- duration: These problems have been building for about seven months now, basically It's hard to say for sure, but I think this is the first time I've ever felt this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any previous episodes like this, or any past periods of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't ever experienced periods of depression or anything quite like this before. This current episode is the first time I've felt this way, and it is definitely the main issue I'm dealing with right now.\n\nDoctor: During this period, how much have you been sleeping, and do you feel fine or even energized despite needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Most nights I'm only sleeping about two or three hours, but I wake up completely buzzing with energy and ideas. I often find myself pacing around writing on my whiteboard at 3 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all—just frustrated that my physical body can't keep up with how fast my mind is spinning.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any hallucinations or felt that your thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or inserted by someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any hallucinations or anything like that. I've also never felt like my thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or put into my head by someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to do things that were clearly out of character or risky, such as spending a lot of money, making impulsive decisions, getting into arguments, or having trouble functioning at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven't had issues with spending money, but I've been getting into constant arguments, snapping at my students and partner, and reacting angrily to colleagues. At home, my partner is totally exhausted and finds me impossible to be around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this current episode of feeling unusually energized, needing much less sleep, and being more irritable lasted without a break? Has it been at least one week, or has it been shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Oh, it has been going on for much longer than a week. These problems have been building continuously for about seven months now, ever since I secured my major grant and took over running the new lab.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods during these seven months when your mood and energy returned to normal for at least a few days, or has it been nonstop the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks; it has been completely nonstop for the entire seven months. It feels like I've been wired to a constant high voltage with absolutely no periods where my mood or energy returned to normal. I just haven't been able to dial it back at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so disorganized, confused, or out of touch with reality during this episode that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized or completely lost touch with reality, but my work and life have become incredibly chaotic and disorganized. While I haven't needed urgent hospital care, my erratic behavior and missed deadlines led my department chair to refer me to occupational health, who then sent me here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?"

This current episode is the first time I've felt this way, and it is definitely the main issue I'm dealing with right now.\n\nDoctor: During this period, how much have you been sleeping, and do you feel fine or even energized despite needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Most nights I'm only sleeping about two or three hours, but I wake up completely buzzing with energy and ideas. I often find myself pacing around writing on my whiteboard at 3 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all—just frustrated that my physical body can't keep up with how fast my mind is spinning.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any hallucinations or felt that your thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or inserted by someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any hallucinations or anything like that. I've also never felt like my thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or put into my head by someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to do things that were clearly out of character or risky, such as spending a lot of money, making impulsive decisions, getting into arguments, or having trouble functioning at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven't had issues with spending money, but I've been getting into constant arguments, snapping at my students and partner, and reacting angrily to colleagues. Because of my chaotic commitments, my lab is in complete disarray, deadlines are missed, and my department chair has warned that my job is at risk. At home, my partner is totally exhausted and finds me impossible to be around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this current episode of feeling unusually energized, needing much less sleep, and being more irritable lasted without a break? Has it been at least one week, or has it been shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Oh, it has been going on for much longer than a week. These problems have been building continuously for about seven months now, ever since I secured my major grant and took over running the new lab.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods during these seven months when your mood and energy returned to normal for at least a few days, or has it been nonstop the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks; it has been completely nonstop for the entire seven months. It feels like I've been wired to a constant high voltage with absolutely no periods where my mood or energy returned to normal. I just haven't been able to dial it back at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so disorganized, confused, or out of touch with reality during this episode that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized or completely lost touch with reality, but my work and life have become incredibly chaotic and disorganized. While I haven't needed urgent hospital care, my erratic behavior and missed deadlines led my department chair to refer me to occupational health, who then sent me here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any hallucinations, and I've never been so confused or disorganized that people couldn't understand what I was saying. I also haven't ever required hospitalization. It's just been incredibly chaotic and stressful, but I haven't lost touch with reality like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: I haven't had any hallucinations or ever needed urgent hospital care. However, my mind has been incredibly chaotic and racing so fast lately that others are having a really hard time keeping up with me or understanding my train of thought. It just feels like my brain is moving too fast for everyone else to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period during this episode where you became so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care, or where other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized and I don't think I've completely lost touch with reality. It's just that my mind has been racing nonstop and things in my life have become incredibly chaotic. I've never been so far gone that I needed that kind of urgent care."

Because of my chaotic commitments, my lab is in complete disarray, deadlines are missed, and my department chair has warned that my job is at risk. At home, my partner is totally exhausted and finds me impossible to be around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this current episode of feeling unusually energized, needing much less sleep, and being more irritable lasted without a break? Has it been at least one week, or has it been shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Oh, it has been going on for much longer than a week. These problems have been building continuously for about seven months now, ever since I secured my major grant and took over running the new lab.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods during these seven months when your mood and energy returned to normal for at least a few days, or has it been nonstop the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks; it has been completely nonstop for the entire seven months. It feels like I've been wired to a constant high voltage with absolutely no periods where my mood or energy returned to normal. I just haven't been able to dial it back at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so disorganized, confused, or out of touch with reality during this episode that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized or completely lost touch with reality, but my work and life have become incredibly chaotic and disorganized. While I haven't needed urgent hospital care, my erratic behavior and missed deadlines led my department chair to refer me to occupational health, who then sent me here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any hallucinations, and I've never been so confused or disorganized that people couldn't understand what I was saying. I also haven't ever required hospitalization. It's just been incredibly chaotic and stressful, but I haven't lost touch with reality like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: I haven't had any hallucinations or ever needed urgent hospital care. However, my mind has been incredibly chaotic and racing so fast lately that others are having a really hard time keeping up with me or understanding my train of thought. It just feels like my brain is moving too fast for everyone else to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period during this episode where you became so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care, or where other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized and I don't think I've completely lost touch with reality. It's just that my mind has been racing nonstop and things in my life have become incredibly chaotic. I've never been so far gone that I needed that kind of urgent care.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care or other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: I have not experienced any hallucinations and have never been hospitalized. This current episode is the first time I've felt this way, and it is definitely the main issue I'm dealing with right now.\n\nDoctor: During this period, how much have you been sleeping, and do you feel fine or even energized despite needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Most nights I'm only sleeping about two or three hours, but I wake up completely buzzing with energy and ideas. I often find myself pacing around writing on my whiteboard at 3 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all—just frustrated that my physical body can't keep up with how fast my mind is spinning.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any hallucinations or felt that your thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or inserted by someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any hallucinations or anything like that. I've also never felt like my thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or put into my head by someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to do things that were clearly out of character or risky, such as spending a lot of money, making impulsive decisions, getting into arguments, or having trouble functioning at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven't had issues with spending money, but I've been getting into constant arguments, snapping at my students and partner, and reacting angrily to colleagues. Because of my chaotic commitments, my lab is in complete disarray, deadlines are missed, and my department chair has warned that my job is at risk. At home, my partner is totally exhausted and finds me impossible to be around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this current episode of feeling unusually energized, needing much less sleep, and being more irritable lasted without a break? Has it been at least one week, or has it been shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Oh, it has been going on for much longer than a week. These problems have been building continuously for about seven months now, ever since I secured my major grant and took over running the new lab.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods during these seven months when your mood and energy returned to normal for at least a few days, or has it been nonstop the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks; it has been completely nonstop for the entire seven months. It feels like I've been wired to a constant high voltage with absolutely no periods where my mood or energy returned to normal. I just haven't been able to dial it back at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so disorganized, confused, or out of touch with reality during this episode that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized or completely lost touch with reality, but my work and life have become incredibly chaotic and disorganized. While I haven't needed urgent hospital care, my erratic behavior and missed deadlines led my department chair to refer me to occupational health, who then sent me here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any hallucinations, and I've never been so confused or disorganized that people couldn't understand what I was saying. I also haven't ever required hospitalization. It's just been incredibly chaotic and stressful, but I haven't lost touch with reality like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: I haven't had any hallucinations or ever needed urgent hospital care. However, my mind has been incredibly chaotic and racing so fast lately that others are having a really hard time keeping up with me or understanding my train of thought. It just feels like my brain is moving too fast for everyone else to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period during this episode where you became so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care, or where other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized and I don't think I've completely lost touch with reality. It's just that my mind has been racing nonstop and things in my life have become incredibly chaotic. I've never been so far gone that I needed that kind of urgent care.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care or other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: I have not experienced any hallucinations and have never been hospitalized. While I do talk very fast and get highly distracted, I haven't been completely out of touch with reality or completely incoherent to others.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so overconfident or energized that you did things that were clearly dangerous or led to major consequences, like spending recklessly, driving dangerously, or acting in a way that put you or others at risk?\n\nPatient: While I haven't engaged in reckless spending or dangerous driving, my extreme overconfidence and energy have definitely led to major consequences at work. I felt like I was operating on a completely different level than everyone else, which led me to impulsively rewrite approved lab protocols on the fly and make chaotic commitments. This has thrown my lab into complete chaos and put my entire career at serious risk."

But I have been absolutely convinced that I’m on the verge of a field-changing scientific breakthrough that only I can see, operating on a completely different level than my colleagues even though they question me and find it ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a previous episode like this before, or a time when you felt very depressed for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don't recall ever having an episode like this before; this level of intensity feels completely new to me. I also don't remember ever having a period of severe depression that lasted for two weeks. It's hard to say for sure, but I think this is the first time I've ever felt this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any previous episodes like this, or any past periods of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't ever experienced periods of depression or anything quite like this before. This current episode is the first time I've felt this way, and it is definitely the main issue I'm dealing with right now.\n\nDoctor: During this period, how much have you been sleeping, and do you feel fine or even energized despite needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Most nights I'm only sleeping about two or three hours, but I wake up completely buzzing with energy and ideas. I often find myself pacing around writing on my whiteboard at 3 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all—just frustrated that my physical body can't keep up with how fast my mind is spinning.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any hallucinations or felt that your thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or inserted by someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any hallucinations or anything like that. I've also never felt like my thoughts were being controlled, broadcast, or put into my head by someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to do things that were clearly out of character or risky, such as spending a lot of money, making impulsive decisions, getting into arguments, or having trouble functioning at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven't had issues with spending money, but I've been getting into constant arguments, snapping at my students and partner, and reacting angrily to colleagues. Because of my chaotic commitments, my lab is in complete disarray, deadlines are missed, and my department chair has warned that my job is at risk. At home, my partner is totally exhausted and finds me impossible to be around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this current episode of feeling unusually energized, needing much less sleep, and being more irritable lasted without a break? Has it been at least one week, or has it been shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Oh, it has been going on for much longer than a week. These problems have been building continuously for about seven months now, ever since I secured my major grant and took over running the new lab.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods during these seven months when your mood and energy returned to normal for at least a few days, or has it been nonstop the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks; it has been completely nonstop for the entire seven months. It feels like I've been wired to a constant high voltage with absolutely no periods where my mood or energy returned to normal. I just haven't been able to dial it back at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so disorganized, confused, or out of touch with reality during this episode that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized or completely lost touch with reality, but my work and life have become incredibly chaotic and disorganized. While I haven't needed urgent hospital care, my erratic behavior and missed deadlines led my department chair to refer me to occupational health, who then sent me here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any hallucinations, and I've never been so confused or disorganized that people couldn't understand what I was saying. I also haven't ever required hospitalization. It's just been incredibly chaotic and stressful, but I haven't lost touch with reality like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had hallucinations, or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand you or you needed urgent hospital care?\n\nPatient: I haven't had any hallucinations or ever needed urgent hospital care. However, my mind has been incredibly chaotic and racing so fast lately that others are having a really hard time keeping up with me or understanding my train of thought. It just feels like my brain is moving too fast for everyone else to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period during this episode where you became so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care, or where other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been hospitalized and I don't think I've completely lost touch with reality. It's just that my mind has been racing nonstop and things in my life have become incredibly chaotic. I've never been so far gone that I needed that kind of urgent care.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any hallucinations or become so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that you needed hospital care or other people could not understand what you were saying?\n\nPatient: I have not experienced any hallucinations and have never been hospitalized. While I do talk very fast and get highly distracted, I haven't been completely out of touch with reality or completely incoherent to others.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so overconfident or energized that you did things that were clearly dangerous or led to major consequences, like spending recklessly, driving dangerously, or acting in a way that put you or others at risk?\n\nPatient: While I haven't engaged in reckless spending or dangerous driving, my extreme overconfidence and energy have definitely led to major consequences at work. I felt like I was operating on a completely different level than everyone else, which led me to impulsively rewrite approved lab protocols on the fly and make chaotic commitments. This has thrown my lab into complete chaos and put my entire career at serious risk.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period during this episode when you became so confused, disorganized, or out of touch with reality that other people could not understand what you were saying or you needed hospital care?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced being completely out of touch with reality, having completely unintelligible speech, or needing hospitalization during this time. My mind has been racing constantly and things have been very chaotic, but it hasn't gotten to that extreme point. I've always been able to make myself understood, even if I'm talking too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been so certain of these beliefs that you ignored clear evidence against them, or had others say your thinking became completely impossible to follow?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely certain about my groundbreaking ideas and dismissed my colleagues' doubts, believing they simply couldn't understand my vision because I was operating on a different level. Additionally, because my mind jumps so rapidly from one thing to another, my students and colleagues have found my train of thought chaotic and hard to follow, even though it feels perfectly clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever completely lost touch with reality during this episode, such as having hallucinations or fixed delusions that others strongly disagreed with, or needing hospital care because of how severe it became?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't had any hallucinations or needed to be hospitalized, but I was referred here by occupational health because of how chaotic things got at work. I have, however, had very intense, grand ideas about a major scientific breakthrough that I was absolutely convinced of, even when my colleagues strongly disagreed with me.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI haven't had any hallucinations or ever needed urgent hospital care.
